                - The temperature in this layer decreases at the rate of 1°C for every 165m of height
                - This is the most important layer for all biological activity
                - Tropopause:
                    - The zone separating the troposphere from stratosphere is known as the tropopause
                    - The air temperature at the tropopause is about -80°C over the equator and about -45°C over the poles
                    - The temperature here is nearly constant, and hence, it is called the tropopause
            2. Stratosphere
                - The stratosphere is found above the tropopause and extends up to a height of 50 km
                - One important feature of the stratosphere is that it contains the ozone layer
                - This layer absorbs ultra-violet radiation and shields life on the earth from intense, harmful form of energy
                - Stratopause:
                    - The boundary between the stratosphere and mesosphere.
                    - The temperature stops increasing and begins to decrease as you move into the mesosphere.
                    - Marks the highest point of the stratosphere, and temperature begins to drop rapidly in the mesosphere.
            3. Mesosphere
                - The mesosphere lies above the stratosphere, which extends up to a height of 80 km
                - In this layer, once again, temperature starts decreasing with the increase in altitude and reaches up to -100°C at the height of 80 km
                - The upper limit of mesosphere is known as the mesopause
                - Mesopause:
                    - The boundary between the mesosphere and thermosphere.
                    - The coldest part of the atmosphere.
                    - Marks the transition to the thermosphere, where temperatures start increasing with altitude.
            4. Thermosphere (Ionosphere)
                - The ionosphere is located between 80 and 400 km above the mesopause
                - It contains electrically charged particles known as ions, and hence, it is known as ionosphere
                - Radio waves transmitted from the earth are reflected back to the earth by this layer
                - Temperature here starts increasing with height
                - Thermopause:
                    - The boundary between the thermosphere and exosphere.
                    - The point at which atmospheric particles are so far apart that they can escape into space.
                    - Temperature starts to increase sharply in the thermosphere but begins to decline in the exosphere.
            5. Exosphere
                - The uppermost layer of the atmosphere above the thermosphere is known as the exosphere
                - This is the highest layer but very little is known about it
                - Whatever contents are there, these are extremely rarefied in this layer, and it gradually merges with the outer space
                - Although all layers of the atmosphere must be exercising influence on us, geographers are concerned with the first two layers of the atmosphere
        - Summary table
            - 

                

                    Layer
                    Reason for Division
                    Characteristics
                

                

                    Troposphere
                    Temperature decreases with altitude; it's where weather phenomena occur.
                    Contains most of Earth's mass (about 75%), water vapor, and is responsible for weather patterns. The temperature decreases with altitude.
                

                

                    Tropopause
                    Acts as a boundary between the troposphere and stratosphere where the temperature stops decreasing and levels out.
                    Temperature stabilizes and then starts to increase with altitude. It marks the upper limit of weather systems.
                

                

                    Stratosphere
                    Temperature increases with altitude due to the absorption of ultraviolet radiation by the ozone layer.
                    Contains the ozone layer that absorbs UV radiation, leading to an increase in temperature. The air is more stable here.
                

                

                    Stratopause
                    Boundary where temperature begins to decrease as we enter the mesosphere.
                    The highest point of the stratosphere. Temperature starts to cool again as we move into the mesosphere.
                

                

                    Mesosphere
                    Temperature decreases with altitude; it is the coldest layer of the atmosphere.
                    This is where most meteorites burn up. Temperature continues to drop with altitude, reaching the lowest temperatures in the atmosphere.
                

                

                    Mesopause
                    Boundary between the mesosphere and thermosphere where temperature starts to rise again.
                    The coldest region of the atmosphere, with temperatures reaching as low as -90°C.
                

                

                    Thermosphere
                    Temperature increases drastically with altitude due to absorption of high-energy solar radiation.
                    This layer contains the ionosphere (important for radio communication). The temperature rises significantly as altitude increases.
                

                

                    Thermopause
                    Boundary between the thermosphere and the exosphere where temperature levels off.
                    Also known as the exobase, where the atmosphere transitions to outer space. The particles are very sparse here.
                

                

                    Exosphere
                    The uppermost layer where atmospheric particles can escape into space.
                    Particles are widely spaced, and the temperature is very high, but there’s no true “boundary” between the exosphere and space.
                

            
            
        - Ionization Zones:
            - The ionosphere is a region in the mesosphere, thermosphere, and exosphere that contains ionized particles (ions and free electrons) due to solar radiation.
        - Elements of weather and climate
            - The main elements of atmosphere which are subject to change and which influence human life on earth are temperature, pressure, winds, humidity, clouds and precipitation
            - These elements have been dealt in detail in Chapters 9, 10 and 11


- Solar radiation, heat balance and temperature
    - Introduction to the atmosphere and energy
        - Overview
            - We inhale and exhale but we feel the air when it is in motion
            - It means air in motion is wind
            - You have already learnt about the fact that earth is surrounded by air all around
            - This envelop of air is atmosphere which is composed of numerous gases
            - These gases support life over the earth’s surface
            - The earth receives almost all of its energy from the sun
            - The earth in turn radiates back to space the energy received from the sun
            - As a result, the earth neither warms up nor does it get cooled over a period of time
            - Thus, the amount of heat received by different parts of the earth is not the same
            - This variation causes pressure differences in the atmosphere
            - This leads to transfer of heat from one region to the other by winds
            - This chapter explains the process of heating and cooling of the atmosphere and the resultant temperature distribution over the earth’s surface

    - Solar radiation
        - Overview
            - The earth’s surface receives most of its energy in short wavelengths
            - The energy received by the earth is known as incoming solar radiation which in short is termed as insolation
                - The term do not refer for whole earch but an area
            - As the earth is a geoid resembling a sphere, the sun’s rays fall obliquely [indirectly / at an angle] at the top of the atmosphere and the earth intercepts [obstruct in between from reaching destination] a very small portion of the sun’s energy
                - Earth is called a geoid because its shape is best described as an irregular, undulating surface that represents the Earth's gravitational field at sea level. 
                - This shape is not a perfect sphere or ellipsoid, but rather a slightly flattened sphere with bumps and dips. This irregular shape arises from variations in the Earth's mass distribution and gravitational pull. 
            - On an average the earth receives 1.94 calories per sq. cm per minute at the top of its atmosphere
        - Insolation
            - Insolation is the amount of solar radiation received by a given area of the Earth's surface per unit time.
            - Key Points:
                - It stands for incoming solar radiation.  
                - Measured in watts per square meter (W/m²).
                - Influences temperature, weather, and climate patterns.
                - Affected by factors like latitude, time of day, season, and atmospheric conditions.
            -  Selective Absorption by Gases
                - Gases in the atmosphere selectively absorb radiation.    
                - They are not efficient at absorbing shortwave solar radiation, but they efficiently absorb and re-emit longwave infrared radiation emitted by Earth.
                - Wavelength Difference
                    - Incoming solar radiation (shortwave):
                        - Mostly visible light and UV rays (wavelength ~0.1 to 4 microns).
                        - These shortwaves pass through the atmosphere relatively easily because atmospheric gases are largely transparent to them.
                    - Outgoing terrestrial radiation (longwave):
                        - Earth emits energy as infrared radiation (wavelength ~4 to 100 microns).
                        - Atmospheric gases like carbon dioxide, water vapor, methane, and ozone are good absorbers of longwave radiation, so they trap and re-radiate this heat — this is the greenhouse effect.
                - Why Earth emits longwave (infrared) radiation instead of shortwave?
                    - This is governed by Planck’s Law and Stefan-Boltzmann Law, which relate temperature to radiation wavelength.
                    - Hotter bodies emit shorter wavelengths while Cooler bodies emit longer wavelengths
                        - When an object gets hotter, the kinetic energy of its particles increases.
                        - These faster-moving particles cause electromagnetic oscillations with higher frequencies.
                        - Higher frequency = shorter wavelength
        - Variations in solar output
            - The solar output received at the top of the atmosphere varies slightly [to a small degree] in a year due to the variations in the distance between the earth and the sun
            - During its revolution around the sun, the earth is farthest from the sun (152 million km) on 4th July
                - This position of the earth is called aphelion
            - On 3rd January, the earth is the nearest to the sun (147 million km)
                - This position is called perihelion
            - Therefore, the annual insolation [the amount of solar radiation reaching a given area] received by the earth on 3rd January is slightly more than the amount received on 4th July
            - However, the effect of this variation in the solar output is masked by other factors like the distribution of land and sea and the atmospheric circulation
            - Hence, this variation in the solar output does not have great effect on daily weather changes on the surface of the earth
            - Common misconception about proximity to sun
                - It’s a common misconception that the Earth’s closest approach to the Sun (perihelion) coincides with the coldest part of the year (winter) and the farthest point (aphelion) coincides with the hottest part of the year (summer), but the seasons are not caused by the Earth’s distance from the Sun. 
                - They are due to the tilt of the Earth’s axis relative to its orbit around the Sun.
                - Despite being closer to the Sun at Perihelion, it is winter in the northern hemisphere. This happens because the Earth is tilted on its axis, and during this time, the northern hemisphere is tilted away from the Sun.
                - Even though the Earth is farther from the Sun during Aphelion, it is summer in the northern hemisphere because the northern hemisphere is tilted toward the Sun.
                - Seasons are caused by the tilt of Earth's axis (about 23.5 degrees) relative to the plane of its orbit, not the Earth’s distance from the Sun.
                - 
        - Variability of insolation [the amount of solar radiation reaching a given area] at the surface of the earth
            - 
            - Overview
                - The amount and the intensity of insolation vary during a day, in a season and in a year
                - Some factors that cause these variations in insolation are: 
                    - The distance from the sun
                    - The rotation of earth on its axis
                    - The angle of inclination of the sun’s rays
                    - The length of the day
                    - The transparency of the atmosphere
                    - The configuration of land in terms of its aspect
                - The last two however, have less influence
            - Earth’s axis and latitude
                - The fact that the earth’s axis makes an angle of 66½ with the plane of its orbit round the sun has a greater influence on the amount of insolation received at different latitudes
            - Angle of inclination
                - The second factor that determines the amount of insolation received is the angle of inclination of the rays
                - This depends on the latitude of a place
                - The higher the latitude the less is the angle they make with the surface of the earth resulting in slant sun rays
                - The area covered by vertical rays is always less than the slant rays
                - If more area is covered, the energy gets distributed and the net energy received per unit area decreases
                - Moreover, the slant rays are required to pass through greater depth of the atmosphere resulting in more absorption, scattering and diffusion
        - The passage of solar radiation through the atmosphere
            - The atmosphere is largely transparent to short wave solar radiation
            - The incoming solar radiation passes through the atmosphere before striking the earth’s surface
            - Within the troposphere water vapour, ozone and other gases absorb much of the near infrared radiation
            - Very small-suspended particles in the troposphere scatter visible spectrum both to the space and towards the earth surface
            - This process adds colour to the sky
                - 
                - Sunlight is white, made up of all colors of the visible spectrum.
                - Blue color
                    - Blue light has a shorter wavelength, so it is scattered much more efficiently (about 10 times more) than red.
                    - Thus, when you look in any direction away from the Sun, the sky appears blue because blue light is scattered in all directions.
                - Red or orange color
                    - At sunrise and sunset, sunlight has to travel a longer path through the atmosphere.
                    - Shorter wavelengths (blue and violet) are scattered out before the light reaches your eyes.
                    - The remaining light is red, orange, or yellow, which has longer wavelengths and is scattered less.
                - White color of clouds
                    - Mie scattering occurs when the particles (like water droplets in clouds) are larger than the wavelength of light.
                    - This type of scattering does not favor any particular wavelength, so all colors scatter equally.
                    - When all colors mix, the result is white.
        - Spatial [relating to or occupying space] distribution of insolation at the earth’s surface
            - The insolation received at the surface varies from about 320 Watt/m2 in the tropics to about 70 Watt/m2 in the poles
            - Maximum insolation is received over the subtropical deserts, where the cloudiness is the least
            - Equator receives comparatively less insolation than the tropics
                - Despite being centrally located between the tropics, the equator receives slightly less annual insolation than the tropics mainly due to
                    - Cloud Cover
                        - The equator is part of the Intertropical Convergence Zone (ITCZ), where warm, moist air rises rapidly.    
                        - This rising air cools and condenses, forming thick cloud cover and frequent rainfall.
                        - These dense clouds reflect or absorb solar radiation, reducing the net insolation reaching the surface.
                    - High Atmospheric Moisture
                        - High humidity in equatorial regions leads to more scattering and absorption of solar radiation in the atmosphere.
                        - This further lowers the direct insolation reaching the ground.
                    - Solar Declination
                        - The Sun is directly overhead twice a year at the equator, but once a year at the tropics.
                        - Around the time of the solstices, the Sun is directly overhead at the Tropic of Cancer (June) and Tropic of Capricorn (December) — leading to maximum insolation there during those periods.
            - Generally, at the same latitude the insolation is more over the continent than over the oceans
            - In winter, the middle and higher latitudes receive less radiation than in summer

    - Heating and cooling of atmosphere
        - Sources of atmospheric heating
            - Primary source of energy for atmospheric heating is the sun, but the atmosphere is not heated directly by by insolation but rather by terrestrial radiation.
            - Terrestrial radiation
                - Terrestrial radiation, also known as outgoing longwave radiation or thermal infrared radiation, is the electromagnetic radiation emitted by the Earth's surface and atmosphere.
                - Earth's atmosphere is mostly heated by terrestrial radiation due to it's inability to absorb short wavelength radiation from insolation.
                - The insolation received by the earth is in short waves forms and heats up its surface
                - The earth after being heated itself becomes a radiating body and it radiates energy to the atmosphere in long wave form
                - This energy heats up the atmosphere from below
                - The long wave radiation is absorbed by the atmospheric gases particularly by carbon dioxide and the other greenhouse gases
                - Thus, the atmosphere is indirectly heated by the earth’s radiation
                - The atmosphere in turn radiates and transmits heat to the space
                - Finally the amount of heat received from the sun is returned to space, thereby maintaining constant temperature at the earth’s surface and in the atmosphere
                - Land emits radiation more quickly and in larger amounts at night because it heats up and cools down faster than oceans while ocean heats slowly and emits slowly and steadily
        - There are different ways of heating and cooling of the atmosphere
            - 
            - Conduction
                - The earth after being heated by insolation transmits the heat to the atmospheric layers near to the earth in long wave form
                - The air in contact with the land gets heated slowly and the upper layers in contact with the lower layers also get heated
                - This process is called conduction
                - Conduction takes place when two bodies of unequal temperature are in contact with one another, there is a flow of energy from the warmer to cooler body
                - The transfer of heat continues until both the bodies attain the same temperature or the contact is broken
                - Conduction is important in heating the lower layers of the atmosphere
                    - The Earth's surface is the primary receiver of solar energy, not the air directly.
                    - This surface (land or water) absorbs solar radiation and becomes warm.
                    - The air molecules in direct contact with the warm surface gain heat through conduction—a process where heat is transferred through direct molecular contact.
                    - Since air is a poor conductor of heat, conduction occurs only in the very thin layer of atmosphere close to the ground (just a few centimeters thick), but it's crucial because it initiates the heating process of the atmosphere.
                    - This warmed air then rises and sets convection in motion, helping to distribute heat further upward.
            - Convection
                - The air in contact with the earth rises vertically on heating in the form of currents and further transmits the heat of the atmosphere
                - This process of vertical heating of the atmosphere is known as convection
                - The convective transfer of energy is confined only to the troposphere
            - Advection
                - The transfer of heat through horizontal movement of air is called advection
                - Horizontal movement of the air is relatively more important than the vertical movement
                - In middle latitudes, most of diurnal (day and night) variation in daily weather are caused by advection alone
                - In tropical regions particularly in northern India during summer season local winds called ‘loo’ is the outcome of advection process
        - Heat budget of the planet earth (radiation budget)
            - 
            - 
            - Figure 9.2 depicts the heat budget of the planet earth
            - The earth as a whole does not accumulate or lose heat
            - It maintains its temperature
            - This can happen only if the amount of heat received in the form of insolation equals the amount lost by the earth through terrestrial radiation
            - Consider that the insolation received at the top of the atmosphere is 100 per cent
            - While passing through the atmosphere some amount of energy is reflected, scattered and absorbed
            - Only the remaining part reaches the earth surface
            - Budget analysis
                - Distribution of Incoming Solar Radiation (100 units)
                    - Reflected back to space: 30 units (Albedo)
                        - 6 units by atmosphere
                        - 20 units by clouds
                        - 4 units by Earth's surface
                    - Absorbed: 70 units
                        - 19 units by atmosphere
                        - 51 units by Earth’s surface
                - Outgoing Terrestrial Radiation
                    - Earth’s Surface:
                        - Absorbs 51 units
                            - Uses part of it for:
                                - Sensible heat transfer (conduction/convection): 7 units
                                - Latent heat transfer (evaporation & transpiration): 23 units
                            - Radiates longwave infrared radiation: 21 units directly to space, 34 units absorbed by atmosphere
                    - Atmosphere:
                        - Absorbs:
                            - 19 units (from solar radiation)
                            - 34 units (from surface longwave radiation)
                            - +11 units from sensible & latent heat transferred from the surface
                            - Total = 64 units
                        - Emits:
                            - 42 units downward to Earth (greenhouse effect)
                            - 22 units upward to space
                - Balance Summary
                    - Incoming = 100 units
                    - Outgoing = 100 units
                        - 30 (reflected)
                        - 21 (radiated directly from Earth’s surface to space)
                        - 22 (radiated from atmosphere to space)
                        - 27 (latent + sensible heat transfers from surface to atmosphere → then radiated to space)
            - This is termed the heat budget or heat balance of the earth
            - This explains, why the earth neither warms up nor cools down despite the huge transfer of heat that takes place
        - Variation in the net heat budget at the earth’s surface
            - 
            - As explained earlier, there are variations in the amount of radiation received at the earth’s surface
            - Some part of the earth has surplus radiation balance while the other part has deficit
            - Figure 9.3 depicts the latitudinal variation in the net radiation balance of the earth — the atmosphere system
            - The figure shows that there is a surplus of net radiation balance between 40 degrees north and south and the regions near the poles have a deficit [shortage / undersupply / deficiency]
            - The surplus heat energy from the tropics is redistributed pole wards and as a result the tropics do not get progressively heated up due to the accumulation of excess heat or the high latitudes get permanently frozen due to excess deficit

    - Temperature
        - Overview
            - The interaction of insolation with the atmosphere and the earth’s surface creates heat which is measured in terms of temperature
            - While heat represents the molecular movement of particles comprising a substance, the temperature is the measurement in degrees of how hot (or cold) a thing (or a place) is
        - Meaning of basic terms
            - Latitude
                - Definition: 
                    - Latitude is the angular distance of a place north or south of the Equator, measured in degrees.
                - Range: 
                    - 0° (Equator) to 90° North (North Pole) and 90° South (South Pole).
                - Lines: 
                    - Lines of latitude (parallels) run horizontally around the globe.
            - Longitude
                - Definition: 
                    - Longitude is the angular distance of a place east or west of the Prime Meridian, measured in degrees.
                - Range: 
                    - 0° (Prime Meridian) to 180° East or West.
                - Lines: 
                    - Lines of longitude (meridians) run vertically from pole to pole.
            - Altitude
                - Altitude is the vertical height of an object or location above sea level.
        - Factors controlling temperature distribution
            - Latitude
                - The temperature of a place depends on the insolation received
                - It has been explained earlier that the insolation varies according to the latitude hence the temperature also varies accordingly
            - Altitude
                - The atmosphere is indirectly heated by terrestrial radiation from below
                - Therefore, the places near the sea-level record higher temperature than the places situated at higher elevations
                - In other words, the temperature generally decreases with increasing height
                - Lapse rate
                    - The rate of decrease of temperature with height is termed as the normal lapse rate
                        - It is 6.5°C per 1,000 m
            - Distance from the sea
                - Another factor that influences the temperature is the location of a place with respect to the sea
                - Compared to land, the sea gets heated slowly and loses heat slowly
                - Land heats up and cools down quickly
                - Therefore, the variation in temperature over the sea is less compared to land
                - The places situated near the sea come under the moderating influence of the sea and land breezes which moderate the temperature
            - Air-mass and ocean currents
                - Like the land and sea breezes, the passage of air masses also affects the temperature
                - The places, which come under the influence of warm air-masses experience higher temperature and the places that come under the influence of cold air-masses experience low temperature
                - Similarly, the places located on the coast where the warm ocean currents flow record higher temperature than the places located on the coast where the cold currents flow
        - Distribution of temperature
            - 
            - 
            - 
            - 
            - Temperature distribution on Earth's surface is not uniform.
            - It is influenced by latitude, land-water contrast, ocean currents, altitude, wind, and cloud cover.
            - Represented on maps using isotherms—lines joining places of equal temperature.
            - Some factors affecting temperature distribution include:
                - Latitude
                - Altitude
                - Land and water contrast
                - Ocean currents
                - Cloud cover and winds
                - Aspect of slope (local)
            - Isothermal zone
                - An isothermal zone refers to a layer in the atmosphere or ocean where temperature remains constant with increasing depth or height.
            - Isotherms
                - Isotherms are imaginary lines drawn on a map or chart that connect places having the same temperature at a given time or during a specific period.
            - January Temperature Distribution
                - Pronounced north-south deviations due to unequal landmass and ocean distribution.
                - Northern Hemisphere:
                    - Greater landmass leads to high continentality.
                    - Isotherms bend equatorward over land and poleward over oceans.
                    - Example: North Atlantic—Isotherms bend northward due to Gulf Stream.
                    - Example: Eurasia—Isotherms bend southward due to intense cooling (e.g., Siberia: -20°C at 50°N and 80°N).
                - Southern Hemisphere:
                    - Ocean-dominated, so temperature variation is smoother.
                    - Isotherms run nearly parallel to latitude lines.
                    - 20°C near 35°S, 10°C near 45°S, and 0°C near 60°S.
            - July Temperature Distribution
                - Seasonal reversal of patterns seen in January.
                - Northern Hemisphere:
                    - Landmasses heat up significantly.
                    - Example: Subtropical Asia shows >30°C.
                    - Isotherms again bend poleward over oceans.
                - Southern Hemisphere:
                    - Oceans remain cooler and show minimal variation.
                    - Isotherms maintain latitudinal pattern.
            - Annual Range of Temperature
                - Highest range: Northeastern Eurasia (>60°C), due to continental climate.
                - Lowest range: Equatorial belt (20°S–15°N), due to constant solar input and oceanic moderation (~3°C).
            - Conclusion
                - Temperature distribution is a result of complex interactions among geographical and climatic factors.
                - Studying isotherms helps understand these patterns and their implications on climate and vegetation.
        - Distribution of Temperature
            - 
            - 
            - 
            - 
            - The global distribution of temperature can well be understood by studying the temperature distribution in January and July.
            - Figure 9.4 (a) and (b) show the distribution of surface air temperature in January and July.
            - Temperature Distribution (Isotherms)
                - The temperature distribution is generally shown on the map with the help of isotherms.
                - Isotherms are lines joining places having equal temperature.
                - In general, the effect of latitude on temperature is well pronounced, as the isotherms are generally parallel to the latitudes.
            - January Distribution
                - The deviation from the general trend is more pronounced in January than in July, especially in the northern hemisphere.
                - In the northern hemisphere, the land surface area is much larger than in the southern hemisphere. Hence, the effects of landmass and ocean currents are more pronounced.
                - In January, the isotherms deviate to the north over the ocean and to the south over the continent. This can be seen in the North Atlantic Ocean.
                - The presence of warm ocean currents, such as the Gulf Stream and North Atlantic Drift, makes the Northern Atlantic Ocean warmer, causing the isotherms to bend northward.
                - Over the land, the temperature decreases sharply, and the isotherms bend southward, particularly in Europe. This effect is more pronounced in the Siberian plain.
                - The mean January temperature along the 60° E longitude is -20°C at both 80° N and 50° N latitudes.
                - The mean monthly temperature for January is:
                    - Over 27°C in equatorial oceans
                    - Over 24°C in the tropics
                    - 2°C to 0°C in the middle latitudes
                    - -18°C to -48°C in the Eurasian continental interior
            - Southern Hemisphere January Distribution
                - The effect of the ocean is well pronounced in the southern hemisphere, where the isotherms are more or less parallel to the latitudes.
                - The variation in temperature is more gradual than in the northern hemisphere.
                - The isotherms of 20°C, 10°C, and 0°C run parallel to 35° S, 45° S, and 60° S latitudes respectively.
            - July Distribution
                - In July, the isotherms generally run parallel to the latitude.
                - The equatorial oceans record warmer temperatures, exceeding 27°C.
                - Over the land, more than 30°C is recorded in the subtropical continental region of Asia, along the 30° N latitude.
                - Along the 40° N latitude, the isotherm of 10°C is observed, and along the 40° S latitude, the temperature is 10°C.
            - Range of Temperature (January and July)
                - Figure 9.5 shows the range of temperature between January and July.
                - The highest range of temperature (more than 60°C) is found over the northeastern part of the Eurasian continent, due to continentality.
                - The least range of temperature (3°C) is found between 20° S and 15° N latitudes.        
        - Inversion of temperature
            - Overview
                - Normally, temperature decreases with increase in elevation
                - It is called normal lapse rate
                - At times, the situations is reversed and the normal lapse rate is inverted
                - It is called inversion of temperature
                - Inversion is usually of short duration but quite common nonetheless
            - Conditions and effects
                - A long winter night with clear skies and still air is ideal situation for inversion
                - The heat of the day is radiated off during the night, and by early morning hours, the earth is cooler than the air above
                - Over polar areas, temperature inversion is normal throughout the year
                - Surface inversion promotes stability in the lower layers of the atmosphere
                - Smoke and dust particles get collected beneath the inversion layer and spread horizontally to fill the lower strata of the atmosphere
                - Dense fogs in mornings are common occurrences especially during winter season
                - This inversion commonly lasts for few hours until the sun comes up and begins to warm the earth
            - Air drainage in hills and mountains
                - The inversion takes place in hills and mountains due to air drainage
                - Cold air at the hills and mountains, produced during night, flows under the influence of gravity
                - Being heavy and dense, the cold air acts almost like water and moves down the slope to pile up deeply in pockets and valley bottoms with warm air above
                - This is called air drainage
                - It protects plants from frost damages
            - Additional concepts
                - Plank’s law states that hotter a body, the more energy it will radiate and shorter the wavelength of that radiation
                - Specific heat is the energy needed to raise the temperature of one gram of substance by one Celsius


- Atmospheric circulation and weather systems
    - Introduction to atmospheric circulation
        - Air expands when heated and gets compressed when cooled
        - This results in variations in the atmospheric pressure
        - The result is that it causes the movement of air from high pressure to low pressure, setting the air in motion
        - You already know that air in horizontal motion is wind
        - Atmospheric pressure also determines when the air will rise or sink
        - The wind redistributes the heat and moisture across the planet, thereby, maintaining a constant temperature for the planet as a whole
        - The vertical rising of moist air cools it down to form the clouds and bring precipitation
        - This chapter has been devoted [very loving / faithful / loyal] to explain the causes of pressure differences, the forces that control the atmospheric circulation, the turbulent pattern of wind, the formation of air masses, the disturbed weather when air masses interact with each other and the phenomenon of violent tropical storms

    - Atmospheric pressure
        - Introduction
            - The weight of a column of air contained in a unit area from the mean sea level to the top of the atmosphere is called the atmospheric pressure
                - Atmospheric pressure is the force exerted by the air per unit area on the surfce of the earth.
            - The atmospheric pressure is expressed in units such as atmospheres, bars, milibars or pascals
                - Meteorologists often use millibars or pascals to measure atmospheric pressure
            - At sea level the average atmospheric pressure is 1,013.2 milibar and it is called 1 atmosphere or 1 bar
            - Due to gravity the air at the surface is denser and hence has higher pressure
            - Air pressure is measured with the help of a mercury barometer or the aneroid barometer
            - The pressure decreases with height
            - At any elevation it varies from place to place and its variation is the primary cause of air motion, i.e. wind which moves from high pressure areas to low pressure areas
        - Vertical variation of pressure
            - 
            - In the lower atmosphere the pressure decreases rapidly with height
                - As one moves up the air gets varified [becoming different, vary, or variegate] and one feels breathless
            - The decrease amounts to about 1 mb for each 10 m increase in elevation
            - It does not always decrease at the same rate
            - The vertical pressure gradient force is much larger than that of the horizontal pressure gradient
            - But, it is generally balanced by a nearly equal but opposite gravitational force
                - Hence, we do not experience strong upward winds
            - 

                

                    Level
                    Pressure (mb)
                    Temperature (°C)
                

                

                    Sea Level
                    1,013.25
