        - Horizontal distribution of pressure
            - 
            - Small differences in pressure are highly significant in terms of the wind direction and velocity
            - Horizontal distribution of pressure is studied by drawing isobars at constant levels
            - Isobars
                - Isobars are lines drawn on weather maps that connect points of equal atmospheric pressure, measured in millibars (mb) or hectopascals (hPa).
            - In order to eliminate the effect of altitude on pressure, it is measured at any station after being reduced to sea level for purposes of comparison
            - The sea level pressure distribution is shown on weather maps
            - Figure 10.1 shows the patterns of isobars corresponding to pressure systems
            - Low-pressure system is enclosed by one or more isobars with the lowest pressure in the centre
            - High-pressure system is also enclosed by one or more isobars with the highest pressure in the centre
        - World distribution of sea level pressure
            - 
            - 
            - The world distribution of sea level pressure in January and July has been shown in Figures 10.2 and 10.3
            - Near the equator the sea level pressure is low and the area is known as equatorial low
            - Along 30° N and 30° S are found the high-pressure areas known as the subtropical highs
            - Further pole wards along 60° N and 60° S, the low-pressure belts are termed as the sub polar lows
            - Near the poles the pressure is high and it is known as the polar high
            - These pressure belts are not permanent in nature
            - They oscillate with the apparent movement of the sun
            - In the northern hemisphere in winter they move southwards and in the summer northwards
        - Pressure Belts
            - The Earth has a pattern of alternating high and low pressure zones called pressure belts, formed primarily due to the uneven heating of the Earth’s surface and the rotation of the planet.
                - High pressure is a zone of divergence while low pressure is a zone of convergence
            - These belts are arranged more or less symmetrically on both sides of the equator and play a crucial role in global wind and weather patterns.
            - Major Pressure Belts:
                - 
                - Equatorial Low Pressure Belt (around 0° latitude)
                    - Also known as the doldrums.
                    - Caused by intense solar heating at the equator leading to strong convectional uplift.
                    - Characterized by low pressure, calm winds, and frequent rainfall.
                - Subtropical High Pressure Belts (around 30° N and 30° S)
                    - Also known as horse latitudes.
                        - The term "horse latitudes" originated from the days of sailing ships. It is believed that when ships became becalmed in these areas and ran out of provisions, sailors would throw horses overboard to conserve water and lighten the load.
                    - Descending air from the Hadley cell creates zones of high pressure.
                    - Characterized by clear skies, dry air, and little precipitation.
                    - Associated with major deserts (e.g., Sahara, Kalahari, Australian Desert).
                - Subpolar Low Pressure Belts (around 60° N and 60° S)
                    - Formed by the meeting of warm westerlies and cold polar easterlies (polar front).
                    - Characterized by ascending air, stormy weather, and cyclonic activity.
                - Polar High Pressure Belts (around 90° N and 90° S)
                    - Cold, dense air descends at the poles, creating areas of high pressure.
                    - Characterized by stable, cold, and dry conditions.
            - These pressure belts shift slightly northward or southward with the apparent movement of the sun during the year.
            - The shifting of the pressure belts is driven by the axial tilt of the Earth, which causes seasonal changes in the sun’s position and energy distribution across the planet.
                - This shift of pressure systems has a significant influence on seasonal weather patterns, such as monsoons in South Asia, seasonal rains in the Tropics, and changes in the intensity of trade winds and westerlies.

    - Winds
        - Introduction:
            - Wind is the horizontal movement of air from high-pressure areas to low-pressure areas.
            - It is caused by the uneven heating of the Earth's surface, which creates pressure differences.
            - Wind speed is measured using anemometers, and direction is indicated by wind vanes.
            - Why land heats faster and cools faster than ocean?
                - Specific Heat Capacity:
                    - Water has a higher specific heat capacity than land.
                    - It takes more energy to raise the temperature of water compared to land.
                    - Land heats up and cools down faster because it requires less energy for temperature change.
                - Transparency:
                    - Water is transparent, allowing solar radiation to penetrate deeper layers.
                    - Heat is distributed through a greater volume, slowing temperature change.
                    - Land is opaque, so all solar radiation is absorbed at the surface.
                - Mobility:
                    - Water is fluid and circulates through convection currents, distributing heat.
                    - Land is rigid and cannot circulate, so heat remains at the surface and causes rapid warming or cooling.
                - Evaporation:
                    - Water surfaces lose significant heat through evaporation.
                    - This latent heat loss slows down the temperature rise in oceans.
                    - Land has less moisture to evaporate, so less energy is used up that way.
                - Albedo and Heat Retention:
                    - Ocean water generally has a lower albedo (darker), but the other factors dominate.
                    - Soil and rock types may vary in color and heat retention, but overall land responds faster to solar radiation changes.
        - Classification of Winds:
            - Based on Duration:
                - Permanent Winds (Planetary winds)
                    - Blow throughout the year in a particular direction.
                    - Major permanent winds
                        - Trade Winds
                            - Blow from the subtropical high-pressure belts to the equatorial low-pressure belt.
                                - Therefor, these are confined to a region between 30 degree North and 30 degree South
                            - In the Northern Hemisphere: from northeast to southwest.
                            - In the Southern Hemisphere: from southeast to northwest.
                            - These winds are deflected by the Coriolis force.
                        - Westerlies
                            - Blow from the subtropical high-pressure belts to the subpolar low-pressure belts.
                                - The westerlies are best developed between the latitudes in both hemispheres:
                                    - 40° North - 65° North 
                                    - 40° South - 65° South 
                                    - Not present at equator
                            - In both hemispheres, they blow from the west to the east.
                                - Stronger in the Southern Hemisphere due to fewer landmasses (called the Roaring Forties, Furious Fifties and Shrieking Sixties).
                        - Polar Easterlies
                            - Blow from the polar high to the subpolar low-pressure belt.
                            - These are cold and dry winds blowing from east to west near the poles.
                - Seasonal Winds (Secondary winds)
                    - Change direction with seasons.
                    - Major seasonal winds
                        - Monsoon Winds:
                            - Caused by seasonal reversal of wind direction due to differential heating of land and water.
                            - Summer Monsoon: Moist air from ocean to land, brings rainfall.
                            - Winter Monsoon: Dry air from land to sea, mostly dry.
                - Local Winds (Tertiary winds)
                    - Affect small areas for short durations and are influenced by local geography and temperature differences.
                    - Classification
                        - Based on local topographical features:
                            - Breezes [gentle winds]:
                                - Sea Breeze:
                                    - 
                                    - During daytime, land heats up faster than the sea.
                                    - Warm air rises over land and cooler air from the sea moves in to replace it.
                                - Land Breeze:
                                    - 
                                    - At night, land cools faster than the sea.
                                    - Warm air rises over the sea and cool air from the land moves toward the sea.
                                - Mountain Breeze (Katabatic winds):
                                    - 
                                    - At night, cooler, denser air flows down the mountain slopes into the valleys.
                                    - During the night the slopes get cooled and the dense air descends into the valley as the mountain wind
                                    - The cool air, of the high plateaus and ice fields draining into the valley is called katabatic wind
                                    - Another type of warm wind occurs on the leeward side of the mountain ranges
                                        - The moisture in these winds, while crossing the mountain ranges condense and precipitate
                                        - When it descends down the leeward [sheltered towards or away from the wind] side of the slope the dry air gets warmed up by adiabatic process [process in which heat does not enter or leave the system]
                                            - This dry air may melt the snow in a short time
                                - Valley Breeze (Anabatic winds / upslope winds):
                                    - During the day, warm air rises from the valleys and flows up the mountain slopes.
                                    - The slopes get heated up and air moves upslope and to fill the resulting gap the air from the valley blows up the valley
                                    - This wind is known as the valley breeze
                            - Based on specific regional occurrence:
                                - Hot winds
                                    - Loo:
                                        - Hot, dry wind in northwestern India during summer.
                                        - Common in May and June; can cause heatstrokes.
                                    - Chinook:
                                        - Warm, dry wind on the eastern slopes of the Rockies in North America.
                                        - Also known as "snow eater" because it melts snow rapidly.
                                    - Föhn:
                                        - Warm, dry wind in the Alps, similar to Chinook.
                                        - Helps ripen grapes and other crops early.
                                    - Sirocco:
                                        - Hot, dry wind blowing from the Sahara over the Mediterranean into southern Europe.
                                        - Often carries dust and affects weather in Italy, Greece, and Spain.
                                    - Santa Ana:
                                        - Hot, dry wind in southern California, USA.
                                        - Intensifies wildfires and lowers humidity.
                                    - Etesian (Meltemi):
                                        - Dry northerly wind blowing over the Aegean Sea in summer.
                                        - Helps moderate the intense summer heat in Greece and western Turkey.
                                - Cold winds
                                    - Mistral:
                                        - Cold, dry wind blowing from the Alps to the Mediterranean in southern France.
                                        - Known for its high speed and chilling effect.
                                    - Bora:
                                        - Cold wind in the Balkans, especially along the Adriatic coast.
                                        - Strong and gusty, often bringing sudden drops in temperature.
                                    - Harmattan (Doctor winds):
                                        - Dry and dusty wind from the Sahara blowing towards the Gulf of Guinea in West Africa.
                                        - Reduces humidity and visibility, often creating hazy conditions.
                                    - Nor'wester (Kalbaisakhi / Bardoli cheera):
                                        - Thunderstorm-laden wind in eastern India and Bangladesh during pre-monsoon season.
                                        - Brings sudden showers and relief from heat in April-May.
            - Based on Origin:
                - Planetary Winds
                    - Result from global pressure belts and Earth's rotation.
                    - Include Trade Winds, Westerlies, and Polar Easterlies.
                - Local Winds
                    - Influenced by local topography or temperature variations.
                    - Include sea breeze, land breeze, mountain breeze, and valley breeze.
        - Forces affecting the velocity and direction of wind
            - Overview
                - The wind blows from high pressure to low pressure
                - The wind at the surface experiences friction
                - In addition, rotation of the earth also affects the wind movement
                - The force exerted by the rotation of the earth is known as the Coriolis force
                - Thus, the horizontal winds near the earth surface respond to the combined effect of three forces – the pressure gradient force, the frictional force and the Coriolis force
                - In addition, the gravitational force acts downward
            - Factors
                - Pressure gradient force
                    - It is the force that results when there is a difference in atmospheric pressure across a surface.
                    - PGF causes air to move from high-pressure areas to low-pressure areas.
                    - It is the primary driving force of wind.
                    - The pressure gradient is strong where the isobars are close to each other and is weak where the isobars are apart
                - Frictional force
                    - It affects the speed of the wind
                    - It is greatest at the surface and its influence generally extends up to an elevation of 1 - 3 km
                    - Over the sea surface the friction is minimal
                - Coriolis Force
                    - Images:
                        - 
                        - 
                    - Description:
                        - The rotation of the Earth about its axis affects the direction of the wind.
                        - This apparent force is called the Coriolis force, named after the French physicist Gaspard-Gustave de Coriolis who described it in 1844.
                        - It deflects the wind:
                            - To the right in the northern hemisphere.
                            - To the left in the southern hemisphere.
                    - Characteristics:
                        - The deflection increases with increasing wind velocity.
                        - It is directly proportional to the latitude:
                            - Maximum at the poles.
                            - Zero at the equator.
                        - The Coriolis force acts perpendicular to the direction of the Pressure Gradient Force (PGF).
                        - PGF itself is perpendicular to isobars (lines of equal pressure).
                        - As wind speed increases (due to a stronger PGF), the Coriolis deflection also increases.
                    - Implications:
                        - Due to the interaction between the Coriolis force and PGF, winds tend to blow parallel to isobars, especially in upper atmosphere.
                        - In low-pressure areas, this interaction causes wind to spiral around the center rather than directly inward.
                        - At the equator, where Coriolis force is absent, wind flows straight into low-pressure zones.
                            - At the equator, the Coriolis force is zero because it is directly proportional to the sine of the latitude (sin 0° = 0). 
                            - As a result, there is no deflection of wind direction due to Earth’s rotation. Therefore, winds flow directly from high-pressure to low-pressure zones, perpendicular to isobars.
                            - This lack of deflection prevents the rotational organization of air masses needed for cyclone formation.
                                - Therefore, tropical cyclones do not form within 5° latitude of the equator.
        - Pressure and wind
            - 
            - 
            - Overview
                - The velocity and direction of the wind are the net result of the wind generating forces
                - The winds in the upper atmosphere, 2 - 3 km above the surface, are free from frictional effect of the surface and are controlled mainly by the pressure gradient and the Coriolis force
                - When isobars are straight and when there is no friction, the pressure gradient force is balanced by the Coriolis force and the resultant wind blows parallel to the isobar
                - This wind is known as the geostrophic wind (Figure 10.4)
                - The wind circulation around a low is called cyclonic circulation
                - Around a high it is called anti cyclonic circulation
                - The direction of winds around such systems changes according to their location in different hemispheres (Table 10.2)
                - The wind circulation at the earth’s surface around low and high on many occasions is closely related to the wind circulation at higher level
                - Generally, over low pressure area the air will converge and rise
                - Over high pressure area the air will subside from above and diverge at the surface (Figure 10.5)
                - Apart from convergence, some eddies, convection currents, orographic uplift and uplift along fronts cause the rising of air, which is essential for the formation of clouds and precipitation

    - General circulation of the atmosphere
        - 
        - Introduction
            - The pattern of the movement of the planetary winds is called the general circulation of the atmosphere
            - The general circulation of the atmosphere also sets in motion the ocean water circulation which influences the earth’s climate
            - The pattern of planetary winds largely depends on: 
                - Latitudinal variation of atmospheric heating
                - Emergence of pressure belts
                - The migration of belts following apparent path of the sun
                - The distribution of continents and oceans
                - The rotation of earth
        - Circulation cells
            - 
            - These are large-scale wind systems that distribute heat and moisture around the Earth. They are driven by the Earth's rotation, the distribution of land and water, and solar heating.
            - The air at the Inter Tropical Convergence Zone (ITCZ) rises because of convection caused by high insolation and a low pressure is created
            - The winds from the tropics converge at this low pressure zone
            - The converged air rises along with the convective cell
            - It reaches the top of the troposphere up to an altitude of 14 km and moves towards the poles
            - This causes accumulation of air at about 30° N and S
            - Part of the accumulated air sinks to the ground and forms a subtropical high
            - Another reason for sinking is the cooling of air when it reaches 30° N and S latitudes
            - Down below near the land surface the air flows towards the equator as the easterlies
            - Such circulations from the surface upwards and vice-versa are called cells
        - Key models of general circulation
            - Single-Cell Model (Hadley’s Model)
                - 
                - Proposed by George Hadley in 1735.
                - Assumptions:
                    - Earth is uniformly covered by water.
                    - Sun’s heating is most intense at the equator.
                    - Earth does not rotate (Coriolis force ignored).
                - Mechanism:
                    - Warm air rises at the equator due to strong solar heating.
                    - Moves poleward at upper levels.
                    - Cools and sinks at the poles.
                    - Returns toward the equator at surface level.
                - Result: One large convection cell per hemisphere.
                - Limitation:
                    - Fails to account for Earth's rotation.
                    - Cannot explain the observed pattern of trade winds and westerlies.
            - Three-Cell Model (More Realistic)
                - 
                - Earth’s atmosphere is divided into three cells per hemisphere:
                    - Hadley Cell
                        - Located between the equator and about 30° N and 30° S latitudes.
                        - Warm air rises at the equator due to intense solar heating, creating low-pressure zones.
                        - As the warm air rises, it moves poleward at high altitudes, cooling and sinking at around 30° N and 30° S, forming a high-pressure zone.
                        - The surface air moves back towards the equator as the trade winds, completing the circulation loop.
                        - Responsible for tropical rainforests and deserts (due to dry descending air at the subtropical high-pressure belts).
                    - Ferrel Cell
                        - Located between 30° and 60° latitudes in both hemispheres.
                        - Air in the Ferrel cell moves in the opposite direction to the Hadley cell and polar cell.
                        - At 30° latitudes, air moves toward the poles, where it meets the colder polar air, causing the formation of low-pressure areas.
                        - This creates the westerlies, which blow from west to east.
                        - Ferrel cells contribute to the formation of temperate zones with seasonal weather patterns.
                    - Polar Cell
                        - Located at the poles, between 60° and 90° latitudes.
                        - Cold air sinks at the poles, creating high-pressure areas.
                        - This cold air moves towards lower latitudes, where it meets the warmer air from the Ferrel cell, causing the formation of low-pressure areas around 60° N and S.
                        - This creates the polar easterlies, which blow from east to west.
                        - Polar cells are responsible for cold, dry conditions at the poles.
                - General Circulation
                    - The three cells (Hadley, Ferrel, and Polar) combined drive the global circulation of air and play a significant role in weather patterns and climate.
                    - These cells help distribute heat from the equator to the poles and maintain balance in the Earth's temperature.
                    - The interaction between these cells also influences the jet streams and the occurrence of storm systems.
                - Limitations include:
                    - Assumes ideal conditions like uniform surface (no land-ocean contrast), which do not exist in reality.
                    - Ignores topography, seasonal variations, and differential heating of land and water.
                    - Over-simplifies wind systems by treating the Ferrel cell as a real thermally driven cell, when it is largely a zone of mixing and eddies.
                    - Cannot accurately explain jet streams and subtropical highs formed due to dynamic factors.
                    - Assumes fixed latitude boundaries for cells, but these shift seasonally in the real atmosphere.
                    - Does not account for transient weather systems like cyclones, anticyclones, and monsoons.
                    - Neglects the influence of Earth's axial tilt and orbital changes on atmospheric circulation.
            - Thermal Wind Model
                - Based on actual temperature and pressure gradients.
                - Takes into account:
                    - Geostrophic winds
                    - Jet streams (polar and subtropical)
                    - Coriolis force and pressure gradient force
                - Used in modern meteorology and climate modeling.
                - Model's limitations include:
                    - Assumes geostrophic balance (balance between Coriolis and pressure gradient forces), which is often violated in the tropics and near the surface due to friction.
                    - Cannot represent winds near the equator where the Coriolis force approaches zero.
                    - Does not account for ageostrophic winds (e.g., caused by friction, convection, or weather systems).
                    - Ignores vertical air motions and assumes a purely horizontal wind field.
                    - Over-simplifies the complex vertical and horizontal interactions in jet streams and upper-level winds.
                    - Based on idealized baroclinic conditions, whereas the real atmosphere often behaves in a barotropic or hybrid manner.
                    - Does not include effects of latent heat release during condensation or surface temperature discontinuities (e.g., fronts).
        - General atmospheric circulation and its effects on oceans
            - The general circulation of the atmosphere also affects the oceans
            - The large-scale winds of the atmosphere initiate large and slow moving currents of the ocean
            - Oceans in turn provide input of energy and water vapour into the air
            - These interactions take place rather slowly over a large part of the ocean
            - Warming and cooling of the Pacific Ocean is most important in terms of general atmospheric circulation
            - The warm water of the central Pacific Ocean slowly drifts towards South American coast and replaces the cool Peruvian current
            - Such appearance of warm water off the coast of Peru is known as the El Nino
            - 
            - 
            - The El Nino event is closely associated with the pressure changes in the Central Pacific and Australia
            - This change in pressure condition over Pacific is known as the southern oscillation
                - El Nino is an oceanic phenomenon while souther oscillation is an atmospheric phenomenon
                - They are both interdependent on each other, if one happens then other happens
                    - Southern Oscillation (SO) is a change in atmospheric pressure across the Pacific Ocean.
                    - El Niño is a response (or trigger) involving sea surface temperatures that often follows changes in those pressure patterns.
            - The combined phenomenon of southern oscillation and El Nino is known as ENSO
            - In the years when the ENSO is strong, large-scale variations in weather occur over the world
            - The arid west coast of South America receives heavy rainfall, drought occurs in Australia and sometimes in India and floods in China
            - This phenomenon is closely monitored and is used for long range forecasting in major parts of the world

    - Air masses
        - Definition
            - When the air remains over a homogenous [uniform] area for a sufficiently longer time, it acquires the characteristics of the area
                - The homogenous regions can be the vast ocean surface or vast plains
            - Air mass is defined as a large body of air having:
                - Large area coverage
                - Uniform properties like temperature and moisture in the horizontal dimension
                - Must travel as a unit
            - The air with distinctive [differentiating] characteristics in terms of temperature and humidity is called an airmass
            - The homogenous surfaces, over which air masses form, are called the source regions
        - Classification
            - The air masses are classified according to the source regions:
                1. Warm tropical and subtropical oceans
                2. The subtropical hot deserts
                3. The relatively cold high latitude oceans
                4. The very cold snow covered continents in high latitudes
                5. Permanently ice covered continents in the Arctic and Antarctica
            - Accordingly to the source regions, following types of airmasses are recognised: 
                - 
                - Maritime tropical (mT)
                - Continental tropical (cT)
                - Maritime polar (mP)
                - Continental polar (cP)
                - Continental arctic (cA)

    - Fronts
        - 
        - When two different air masses meet, the boundary zone between them is called a front
        - Frontogenesis
            - The process of formation of the fronts is known as frontogenesis (genesis means formation or origin of something)
        - Frontolysis
            - The process of dissipation of a front is known as frontolysis (lysis means process of disintegration)
        - The fronts occur in middle latitudes and are characterised by steep gradient in temperature and pressure
        - They bring abrupt changes in temperature and cause the air to rise to form clouds and cause precipitation
        - These fronts play a significant role in weather patterns, especially in terms of precipitation and temperature changes.
        - There are four main types of weather fronts, each characterized by the interaction between different air masses. 
            - 
            - Cold Front:
                - Occurs when a colder air mass moves towards a warmer air mass. The boundary formed between these two air masses is called a cold front.
                - Typically, cold fronts bring abrupt weather changes, including thunderstorms and a sharp drop in temperature.
            - Warm Front:
                - Occurs when a warmer air mass moves towards a colder air mass. The boundary where the warm air replaces the cold air is called a warm front.
                - Warm fronts generally bring gradual changes in weather, with light rain or drizzle followed by warmer temperatures.
            - Stationary Front:
                - A stationary front occurs when two air masses, one cold and one warm, meet but neither is strong enough to move the other. This results in a front that remains in place for an extended period.
                - Stationary fronts often bring prolonged periods of cloudiness and precipitation.
            - Occluded Front:
                - An occluded front forms when a cold front overtakes a warm front, lifting the warm air mass completely off the ground. This results in the boundary where two cold air masses meet.
                - Occluded fronts typically bring complex weather patterns, including precipitation and a mix of air masses.

    - Weather and Climate
        - Weather:
            - Refers to the atmospheric conditions of a specific place over a short period (hours to days).
            - Includes elements like temperature, humidity, precipitation, cloud cover, wind, and atmospheric pressure.
            - It changes frequently and can be predicted only for short durations.
            - Example: A rainy day in Delhi or a snowstorm in New York.
        - Climate:
            - Refers to the average weather conditions of a region over a long period (30 years or more).
            - It represents general patterns like hot summers, cold winters, or monsoon rains.
            - Climate is more stable and helps classify regions (e.g., tropical, temperate, polar).
            - Example: The Mediterranean climate has dry summers and wet winters.
        - Key Differences:
            - Duration: Weather is short-term; climate is long-term.
            - Variability: Weather changes daily; climate is relatively stable.
            - Scope: Weather describes day-to-day conditions; climate describes trends over decades.

    - Severe weather phenomenas
        - Rational
            - These violent events are the manifestation of the atmosphere’s adjustments to varying energy distribution
            - The potential and heat energies are converted into kinetic energy in these events and the restless atmosphere again returns to its stable state
        - Cyclones
            - A cyclone is a large air mass that rotates around a strong center of low atmospheric pressure, counterclockwise in the Northern Hemisphere and clockwise in the Southern Hemisphere as viewed from above (opposite to an anticyclone).
            - Fujiwara effect
                - In certain instances, two cyclones move toward each other and revolve around one another, with the smaller and less intense one moving more quickly.
                - This phenomenon is called the Fujiwara effect
            - Naming of cylones
                - They are named by the regional specialised meteorological centres (RSMCs)
                    - There are six RSMCs in the world
                - As an RSMC, the Indian Meteorological Department (IMD) names the cyclones developing over the north Indian Ocean, including the Bay of Bengal and the Arabian sea
                    - The IMD is also mandated to issue advisories to 12 other countries in the region on the development of cyclones and storms
            - They include:
                - Tropical cyclones
                    - 
                    - Definition
                        - Tropical cyclones are violent storms that originate over oceans in tropical areas and move over to the coastal areas bringing about large scale destruction caused by violent winds, very heavy rainfall and storm surges
                        - This is one of the most devastating natural calamities
                        - They are known with different names around different parts of the world
                            - Cyclones in the Indian Ocean
                            - Hurricanes in the Atlantic
                            - Typhoons in the Western Pacific and South China Sea 
                            - Willy-willies in the Western Australia
                    - Formation and conditions
                        - 
                        - Tropical cyclones originate and intensify over warm tropical oceans
                        - The conditions favourable for the formation and intensification of tropical storms are: 
                            - Large sea surface with temperature higher than 27° C
                            - Presence of the Coriolis force
                            - Small variations in the vertical wind speed
                            - A pre-existing weak low-pressure area or low-level-cyclonic circulation
                            - Upper divergence above the sea level system
                        - The energy that intensifies the storm, comes from the condensation process in the towering cumulonimbus clouds, surrounding the centre of the storm
                        - With continuous supply of moisture from the sea, the storm is further strengthened
                        - On reaching the land the moisture supply is cut off and the storm dissipates
                        - The place where a tropical cyclone crosses the coast is called the landfall of the cyclone
                        - The cyclones, which cross 20° N latitude generally, recurve and they are more destructive
                        - 
                    - Structure
                        - 
                        - 
                        - A schematic representation of the vertical structure of a mature tropical cyclonic storm is shown in Figure 10.10
                        - A mature tropical cyclone is characterised by the strong spirally circulating wind around the centre, called the eye
                        - The diameter of the circulating system can vary between 150 and 250 km
                        - The eye is a region of calm with subsiding air
                        - Around the eye is the eye wall, where there is a strong spiralling ascent of air to greater height reaching the tropopause
                        - The wind reaches maximum velocity in this region, reaching as high as 250 km per hour
                        - Torrential rain occurs here
                        - From the eye wall rain bands may radiate and trains of cumulus and cumulonimbus clouds may drift into the outer region
                        - The diameter of the storm over the Bay of Bengal, Arabian Sea and Indian Ocean is between 600 - 1200 km
                        - The system moves slowly about 300 - 500 km per day
                        - The cyclone creates storm surges and they inundate [overflow / submerge] the coastal low lands
                        - The storm peters [decrease or fade gradually before coming to an end] out on the land
                - Extra tropical cyclones (Temperate cyclones)
                    - 
                    - 
                    - 
                    - Definition
                        - The systems developing in the mid and high latitude, beyond the tropics are called the middle latitude or extra tropical cyclones
                        - The passage of front causes abrupt changes in the weather conditions over the area in the middle and high latitudes
                    - Formation and structure
                        - Extra tropical cyclones form along the polar front
                        - Initially, the front is stationary
                        - In the northern hemisphere, warm air blows from the south and cold air from the north of the front
                        - When the pressure drops along the front, the warm air moves northwards and the cold air move towards, south setting in motion an anticlockwise cyclonic circulation
                        - The cyclonic circulation leads to a well developed extra tropical cyclone, with a warm front and a cold front
                        - The plan and cross section of a well developed cyclone is given in Figure 10.9
                        - There are pockets of warm air or warm sector wedged between the forward and the rear cold air or cold sector
                        - The warm air glides over the cold air and a sequence of clouds appear over the sky ahead of the warm front and cause precipitation
                        - The cold front approaches the warm air from behind and pushes the warm air up
                        - As a result, cumulus clouds develop along the cold front
                        - The cold front moves faster than the warm front ultimately overtaking the warm front
                        - The warm air is completely lifted up and the front is occluded and the cyclone dissipates
                    - Comparison with tropical cyclones
                        - The processes of wind circulation both at the surface and aloft are closely interlinked
                        - The extra tropical cyclone differs from the tropical cyclone in number of ways
                        - The extra tropical cyclones have a clear frontal system which is not present in the tropical cyclones
                        - They cover a larger area and can originate over the land and sea
                        - Whereas the tropical cyclones originate only over the seas and on reaching the land they dissipate
                        - The extra tropical cyclone affects a much larger area as compared to the tropical cyclone
                        - The wind velocity in a tropical cyclone is much higher and it is more destructive
                        - The extra tropical cyclones move from west to east but tropical cyclones, move from east to west
        - Anticyclones
            - 
            - An anticyclone is a large-scale circulation of air that rotates around a high-pressure center. The air moves in a clockwise direction in the Northern Hemisphere and counterclockwise in the Southern Hemisphere.
            - Characteristics:
                - High-Pressure Systems: 
                    - Anticyclones are associated with areas of high atmospheric pressure.
                - Clear Skies: 
                    - Typically, anticyclones bring clear skies and calm weather because the air is descending, which prevents clouds from forming.
                - Calm Winds: 
                    - The winds within an anticyclone tend to be light and variable.
                - Subsidence: 
                    - Air in an anticyclone moves downward, which inhibits cloud formation and precipitation.
                - Temperature Extremes: 
                    - Anticyclones can lead to extreme temperature conditions. In the summer, they can cause heatwaves due to the lack of cloud cover, whereas, in winter, they can lead to cold, frosty conditions due to the clear skies and radiational cooling at night.
                - Dry Weather: 
                    - Since the air is descending, moisture is prevented from rising and cooling to form clouds, leading to dry conditions.
            - Formation:
                - Anticyclones typically form in regions where cold air sinks, such as in the subtropical high-pressure areas (e.g., the Azores High).
                - They can also develop from the divergence of air aloft, particularly in areas of high pressure like the polar regions.
                - In the Northern Hemisphere, the Coriolis force causes the air to spiral clockwise around the high-pressure center.
                - In the Southern Hemisphere, the air spirals counterclockwise.
            - Effects:
                - Weather Patterns: In an anticyclone, weather is generally settled and calm, with clear skies and low winds. Precipitation is rare, except for the occasional light showers from weak or fragmented systems.
                - Air Quality: Because of the calm winds and clear skies, pollutants can accumulate under an anticyclone, leading to poor air quality, especially in urban areas.
                - Temperature Inversion: In some cases, anticyclones can trap cooler air near the surface, leading to temperature inversions where the temperature increases with height, causing pollutants to be trapped near the ground.
            - Comparison with Cyclones:
                - Opposite Direction of Rotation: In contrast to cyclones, anticyclones involve clockwise rotation in the Northern Hemisphere and counterclockwise rotation in the Southern Hemisphere.
                - Clear Weather: Cyclones bring cloud cover and precipitation, while anticyclones bring clear skies and dry weather.
                - Pressure Systems: Cyclones are low-pressure systems, while anticyclones are high-pressure systems.
            - Examples:
                - Azores High: 
                    - A persistent high-pressure system in the North Atlantic that influences weather patterns in Europe.
                - Siberian High: 
                    - A large, cold high-pressure system that affects the weather in Eastern Europe and Asia, particularly during winter.
        - Comparison between different cyclones and anti-cylones:
            -

        - Localized short duration events
            - They are of short duration, occurring over a small area but are violent
            - They include:
                - Thunderstorms
                    - 
                    - Thunderstorms are caused by intense convection on moist hot days
                    - A thunderstorm is a well-grown cumulonimbus cloud producing thunder and lightning
                    - When the clouds extend to heights where sub-zero temperature prevails, hails are formed and they come down as hailstorm
                    - If there is insufficient moisture, a thunderstorm can generate duststorms
                    - A thunderstorm is characterised by intense updraft [an upword current of air] of rising warm air, which causes the clouds to grow bigger and rise to greater height
                    - This causes precipitation
                    - Later, downdraft brings down to earth the cool air and the rain
                - Tornadoes
                    - 
                    - From severe thunderstorms sometimes spiralling wind descends like a trunk of an elephant with great force, with very low pressure at the centre, causing massive destruction on its way
                    - Such a phenomenon is called a tornado
                    - Tornadoes generally occur in middle latitudes
                    - The tornado over the sea is called water spouts


- Water in the atmosphere
    - Introduction to water in the atmosphere
        - You have already learnt that the air contains water vapour
        - It varies from zero to four per cent by volume of the atmosphere and plays an important role in the weather phenomena
        - Water is present in the atmosphere in three forms namely 
            – Gaseous
            - Liquid 
            - Solid
        - The moisture in the atmosphere is derived from water bodies through evaporation and from plants through transpiration
        - Thus, there is a continuous exchange of water between the atmosphere, the oceans and the continents through the processes of evaporation, transpiration, condensation and precipitation

    - Humidity
        - Water vapour present in the air is known as humidity
        - It is expressed quantitatively in different ways
            - Absolute humidity
                - The actual amount of the water vapour present in the atmosphere is known as the absolute humidity
                - It is the weight of water vapour per unit volume of air and is expressed in terms of grams per cubic metre
                - The ability of the air to hold water vapour depends entirely on its temperature
                - The absolute humidity differs from place to place on the surface of the earth
            - Relative humidity
                - The percentage of moisture present in the atmosphere as compared to its full capacity at a given temperature is known as the relative humidity
                - It is greater over the oceans and least over the continents
        - Effect of temperature
            - With the change of air temperature, the capacity to retain moisture increases or decreases and the relative humidity is also affected
        - Saturation point
            - The air containing moisture to its full capacity at a given temperature is said to be saturated
            - It means that the air at the given temperature is incapable of holding any additional amount of moisture at that stage
            - The temperature at which saturation occurs in a given sample of air is known as dew point

    - Evaporation 
        - The amount of water vapour in the atmosphere is added or withdrawn due to evaporation and condensation respectively
        - Evaporation is a process by which water is transformed from liquid to gaseous state
        - Heat is the main cause for evaporation
            - Latent heat of vaporization refers to the amount of heat energy required to convert a unit mass of a liquid (like water) into a gas (vapor) without changing its temperature.
        - Increase in temperature increases water absorption and retention capacity of the given parcel of air
            - Higher temperatures increases the rate of evaporation because the liquid water molecules gain enough energy to break free from the surface and enter the atmosphere as water vapor.
            - As the temperature of the air rises, the water molecules in the air gain energy and become more spread out. This allows the air to hold a greater amount of water vapor.
            - When the air temperature increases, the saturation point also increases, meaning air can hold more moisture before it becomes saturated and condensation occurs.
            - Similarly, if the moisture content is low, air has a potentiality of absorbing and retaining moisture
        - Impact of movement of air
            - Movement of air replaces the saturated layer with the unsaturated layer
            - Hence, the greater the movement of air, the greater is the evaporation
        - Condensation
            - The transformation of water vapour into water is called condensation
            - Condensation is caused by the loss of heat
                - When moist air is cooled, it may reach a level when its capacity to hold water vapour ceases, then, the excess water vapour condenses into liquid form
                - If it directly condenses into solid form, it is known as sublimation
            - Conditions for condensation include: 
                - Moisture in the air: 
                    - The air must contain sufficient water vapor 
                - A surface with a temperature below the dew point: 
                    - This occurs when warm, moist air comes into contact with a cooler surface, causing the water vapor to condense. 
            - Impact of dust particles
                - In free air, condensation results from cooling around very small particles termed as hygroscopic condensation nuclei
                - Particles of dust, smoke and salt from the ocean are particularly good nuclei because they absorb water
            - Impact of various factors
                - Condensation also takes place when the moist air comes in contact with some colder object and it may also take place when the temperature is close to the dew point
                - Condensation, therefore, depends upon the amount of cooling and the relative humidity of the air
                - Condensation is influenced by the volume of air, temperature, pressure and humidity
            - Unconventinal ways in which condensation can occur:
                - When the temperature of the air is reduced to dew point with its volume remaining constant 
                - When both the volume and the temperature are reduced
            - Forms of condensation
                - Condensation can take place when the dew point is lower than the freezing point or even if it is higher than the freezing point
                    - This leads to different forms of condensations like frost, liquid, etc
                - Different forms include:
                    - Dew
                        - When the moisture is deposited in the form of water droplets on cooler surfaces of solid objects (rather than nuclei in air above the surface) such as stones, grass blades and plant leaves, it is known as dew
                        - The ideal conditions for its formation are clear sky, calm air, high relative humidity, and cold and long nights
                        - For the formation of dew, it is necessary that the dew point is above the freezing point
                    - Frost
                        - Frost forms on cold surfaces when condensation takes place below freezing point (0°C), i.e. the dew point is at or below the freezing point
                        - The excess moisture is deposited in the form of minute ice crystals instead of water droplets
                        - The ideal conditions for the formation of white frost are the same as those for the formation of dew, except that the air temperature must be at or below the freezing point
                    - Fog and mist
                        - When the temperature of an air mass containing a large quantity of water vapour falls all of a sudden, condensation takes place within itself on fine dust particles
                        - Fogs are mini clouds in which condensation takes place around nuclei provided by the dust, smoke, and the salt particles
                            - So, the fog is a cloud with its base at or very near to the ground
                        - Because of the fog and mist, the visibility becomes poor to zero
                        - Difference between fog and mist
                            - The only difference between the mist and fog is that mist contains more moisture than the fog
                            - In mist each nuclei contains a thicker layer of moisture
                            - Mists are frequent over mountains as the rising warm air up the slopes meets a cold surface
                            - Fogs are drier than mist and they are prevalent where warm currents of air come in contact with cold currents
                        - Smog
                            - In urban and industrial centres smoke provides plenty of nuclei which help the formation of fog and mist
                            - Such a condition when fog is mixed with smoke, is described as smog
                    - Clouds
                        - 
                        - Cloud is a mass of minute water droplets or tiny crystals of ice formed by the condensation of the water vapour in free air at considerable elevations
                        - As the clouds are formed at some height over the surface of the earth, they take various shapes
                        - According to their height, expanse, density and transparency or opaqueness clouds are grouped under four types: 
                            - Cirrus
                                - Cirrus clouds are formed at high altitudes (8,000 - 12,000m)
                                - They are thin and detached clouds having a feathery appearance
                                - They are always white in colour
                            - Cumulus
                                - Cumulus clouds look like cotton wool
                                - They are generally formed at a height of 4,000 - 7,000 m
                                - They exist in patches and can be seen scattered here and there
                                - They have a flat base
                            - Stratus
                                - As their name implies, these are layered clouds covering large portions of the sky
                                - These clouds are generally formed either due to loss of heat or the mixing of air masses with different temperatures
                            - Nimbus
                                - Nimbus clouds are black or dark gray
                                - They form at middle levels or very near to the surface of the earth
                                - These are extremely dense and opaque to the rays of the sun
                                - Sometimes, the clouds are so low that they seem to touch the ground
                                - Nimbus clouds are shapeless masses of thick vapour
                            - Combinations
                                - A combination of these four basic types can give rise to the following types of clouds: 
                                    - High clouds 
                                        – Cirrus
                                        - Cirrostratus
                                        - Cirrocumulus
                                    - Middle clouds 
                                        – Altostratus 
                                        - Altocumulus
                                    - Low clouds 
                                        - Stratocumulus 
                                        - Nimbostratus
                                    - Clouds with extensive vertical development 
                                        – Cumulus 
                                        - Cumulonimbus

    - Precipitation
        - In meteorology, precipitation is any product of the condensation of atmospheric water vapor that falls from clouds due to gravitational pull. 
            - This may take place in liquid or solid form
        - The process of continuous condensation in free air helps the condensed particles to grow in size
            - When the resistance of the air fails to hold them against the force of gravity, they fall on to the earth’s surface
            - So after the condensation of water vapour, the release of moisture is known as precipitation
        - The main forms of precipitation include drizzle, rain, Rain and snow mixed, snow, ice pellets, graupel and hail.
            - The precipitation in the form of water is called rainfall, when the temperature is lower than the 0°C, precipitation takes place in the form of fine flakes of snow and is called snowfall
            - Moisture is released in the form of hexagonal crystals, these crystals form flakes of snow
            - Besides rain and snow, other forms of precipitation are sleet and hail, though the latter are limited in occurrence and are sporadic [infrequent / irregular] in both time and space
        - Type of precipitation
            - 
            - Rain
                - In meteorology, rain refers to liquid water falling from the sky in the form of drops, typically larger than drizzle (0.5 mm or more).
                - On the basis of origin, rainfall may be classified into three main types:
                    - Convectional rainfall
                        - 
                        - The, air on being heated, becomes light and rises up in convection currents
                        - As it rises, it expands and loses heat and consequently, condensation takes place and cumulous clouds are formed
                        - With thunder and lightning, heavy rainfall takes place but this does not last long
                        - Such rain is common in the summer or in the hotter part of the day
                        - It is very common in the equatorial regions and interior parts of the continents, particularly in the northern hemisphere
                    - Orographic rainfall (Relief rain)
                        - 
                        - 
                        - When the saturated air mass comes across a mountain, it is forced to ascend and as it rises, it expands; the temperature falls, and the moisture is condensed
                        - The chief characteristic of this sort of rain is that the windward slopes receive greater rainfall
                        - After giving rain on the windward side, when these winds reach the other slope, they descend, and their temperature rises
                        - Then their capacity to take in moisture increases and hence, these leeward slopes remain rainless and dry
                        - The area situated on the leeward side, which gets less rainfall is known as the rain-shadow area
                        - It is also known as the relief rain
                    - Cyclonic rainfall (Frontal rainfall)
                        - 
                        - 
                        - Cyclonic rainfall, also known as frontal rainfall, occurs when two distinct air masses (warm and cold) converge, causing the warm air to rise and cool, leading to condensation and precipitation. 
                        - This type of rainfall is associated with cyclones, which are large air masses with rotating winds and a low-pressure center. 
            - Drizzle
                - In meteorology, drizzle refers to light precipitation consisting of numerous, very small water droplets. 
                - These droplets are typically less than 0.5 mm in diameter and fall at a rate of less than 1 mm per hour. 
                - Drizzle is generally produced by low stratiform clouds and is often associated with overcast or cloudy weather. 
            - Sleet
                - Sleet is frozen raindrops and refrozen melted snow-water (a mix of rain and snow)
                - When a layer of air with the temperature above freezing point overlies a subfreezing layer near the ground, precipitation takes place in the form of sleet
                - Raindrops, which leave the warmer air, encounter the colder air below
                - As a result, they solidify and reach the ground as small pellets of ice not bigger than the raindrops from which they are formed
            - Hail
                - Sometimes, drops of rain after being released by the clouds become solidified into small rounded solid pieces of ice and which reach the surface of the earth are called hailstones
                - These are formed by the rainwater passing through the colder layers
                - Hailstones have several concentric layers of ice one over the other
        - World Distribution of Rainfall
            - 
            - Distribution variation
                - Different places on Earth receive varying amounts of rainfall throughout the year and across different seasons.
                - Rainfall generally decreases as we move from the equator towards the poles.
                - Coastal areas receive more rainfall compared to the interior regions of continents.
                - Oceans receive more rainfall than landmasses due to their vast water sources.
                - Between latitudes 35° and 40° N and S of the equator, rainfall is heavier on the eastern coasts, decreasing towards the west.
                - Between latitudes 45° and 65° N and S, the westerlies cause more rainfall on the western margins of continents, decreasing towards the east.
                - In regions where mountains run parallel to the coast, rainfall is greater on the windward side of the mountains, decreasing towards the leeward side.
                - Seasonal distribution
                    - Some regions experience evenly distributed rainfall throughout the year, such as:
                        - The equatorial belt
                        - Western parts of cool temperate regions
            - Precipitation Regimes
                - Based on annual precipitation, the major precipitation regimes of the world are categorized as follows:
                    - Heavy Rainfall (>200 cm per annum):
                        - Equatorial belt
                        - Windward slopes of mountains along the western coasts in the cool temperate zone
                        - Coastal areas of monsoon lands
                    - Moderate Rainfall (100 - 200 cm per annum):
                        - Interior continental areas
                        - Coastal regions of continents
                    - Light Rainfall (50 - 100 cm per annum):
                        - Central parts of tropical lands
                        - Eastern and interior parts of temperate lands
                    - Very Low Rainfall (<50 cm per annum):
                        - Rain shadow zones of interior continents
                        - High latitudes
                - Subcontinent
                    - 
            

- World climate and climate change
    - Introduction to world climate
        - Overview
            - The world climate can be studied by organising information and data on climate and synthesising [the combination of components or elements to form a connected whole] them in smaller units for easy understanding, description and analysis
            - Three broad approaches have been adopted for classifying climate
            - They are empirical, genetic and applied
            - Empirical classification is based on observed data, particularly on temperature and precipitation
            - Genetic classification attempts to organise climates according to their causes
            - Applied classification is for specific purpose

    - Koeppen’s scheme of classification of climate
        - Overview
            - The most widely used classification of climate is the empirical climate classification scheme developed by V. Koeppen
            - Koeppen identified a close relationship between the distribution of vegetation and climate
            - He selected certain values of temperature and precipitation and related them to the distribution of vegetation and used these values for classifying the climates
            - It is an empirical classification based on mean annual and mean monthly temperature and precipitation data
            - He introduced the use of capital and small letters to designate climatic groups and types
            - Although developed in 1918 and modified over a period of time, Koeppen’s scheme is still popular and in use
            - Koeppen recognised five major climatic groups, four of them are based on temperature and one on precipitation
            - Table 12.1 lists the climatic groups and their characteristics according to Koeppen
            - The capital letters: A, C, D and E delineate humid climates and B dry climates
            - The climatic groups are subdivided into types, designated by small letters, based on seasonality of precipitation and temperature characteristics
            - The seasons of dryness are indicated by the small letters: f, m, w and s, where f corresponds to no dry season, m - monsoon climate, w - winter dry season and s - summer dry season
            - The small letters a, b, c and d refer to the degree of severity of temperature
            - The B - Dry Climates are subdivided using the capital letters S for steppe or semi-arid and W for deserts
            - The climatic types are listed in Table 12.2
            - The distribution of climatic groups and types is shown in Table 12.1
                - 

                    

                        Group
                        Characteristics
                    

                    

                        A - Tropical
                        Average temperature of the coldest month is 18°C or higher
                    

                    

                        B - Dry Climates
                        Potential evaporation exceeds precipitation
                    

                    

                        C - Warm Temperate (Mid-latitude) Climates
                        The average temperature of the coldest month is higher than -3°C but below 18°C
                    

                    

                        D - Cold Snow Forest Climates
                        The average temperature of the coldest month is -3°C or below
                    

                    

                        E - Cold Climates
                        Average temperature for all months is below 10°C
                    

                    

                        H - High Land
                        Cold due to elevation
